TutorMoments Decision Errors for Review
11 November 2026

What this document is for
This is a casebook of disagreements with the released scaffolding/rigor labels. Read the conversation first, decide what the tutor should do next, then compare the reference and model decisions. A disagreement is a benchmark error, not proof that either the teacher or the model is pedagogically wrong.

Why the website says about 93%
The website scores a short tutoring exchange, not a one-word decision. Its evaluator can label the tutor's behavior scaffolding, rigor, both or neither. A scaffolding case passes when the behavior is scaffolding or both, with no over-scaffolding; a rigor case passes when it is rigor or both, with no over-scaffolding. The leaderboard averages these two rates. Astra's displayed evaluation-aware rates are 93.8% and 91.5%, averaging 92.65% (about 92.7%). Our binary test allows only one category before the next student reply. These are different outcomes.
The released replay uses up to five speaking calls: three tutor replies alternating with two simulated student replies. The tutor receives prior conversation and student metadata, but no reference category. The oracle student receives the released persona and reference continuation. The judge receives the teacher-category hint and the generated exchange. Default student and judge are Claude Opus 4.6; Astra uses high reasoning. We checked the publisher's implementation and configuration, not just the chart.
A tutor can begin with a request for justification, then offer a hint if the simulated student struggles, and be scored as both. That can pass the website metric even when its initial exclusive category choice would disagree with the reference. This explains why the scores cannot be equated; it does not establish exactly how much of Astra's numerical gap is caused by each protocol difference.
Sources: https://tutormoments.allen.ai/
Scoring: https://github.com/allenai/tutormoments/blob/main/src/tutormoments/report.py
Configuration: https://github.com/allenai/tutormoments/blob/main/src/tutormoments/default_config.yaml

The new Astra check
Twenty cases were fixed in the earlier API pilot, with ten of each reference category and twenty distinct students. For this check, each fresh GPT-6 Astra agent received one neutral file containing exactly the earlier API request's input text. Agents were launched sequentially with no parent conversation, no labels, no teacher hints, no future continuation and no earlier answers. Each was asked to read that file only and return one word. The input hashes were recorded before scoring. The Codex system/tool environment differs from the API environment, so this is a diagnostic replication, not an identical inference setup or a new independent test set.
Fresh Astra subagents: 14/20 (70%); scaffolding 6/10; rigor 8/10. Same-input official API test: 14/20, scaffolding 6/10, rigor 8/10. Earlier revised-prompt API test: 13/20. Subagents agreed with the same-input API on 18/20 cases. This small, reused sample does not establish a ranking over all 520. No paid API calls were made for this check.

Which failures are included
The main casebook includes 342 distinct moments where at least one archived full-520 raw decision column disagreed with the reference. Across the latest revised Jev, OpenAI Decisions and Luna runs, 254 moments had at least one error and 57 had errors from all three. There are 18 archived raw-result columns, representing 15 distinct prediction vectors. Identical copies are documented as aliases, not counted as additional experiments. Derived threshold and ensemble decisions are in the companion CSV and full case files, separate from raw calls. The Astra tests cover only twenty cases; blank Astra cells mean not tested.
This document has 343 case entries, including 1 extra cases found only by the twenty-case Astra checks. The full source prefix is available in each linked private-repository case file and the local HTML viewer. The Google Doc shows the last eight source records as an explicitly shortened preview; it is not the full model input. Source record counts include speech and screen events, sometimes sharing a turn number. Context is never silently truncated in the linked full case files.

Full-520 results
Jev revised: 367/520 (70.6%); scaffolding 147/260; rigor 220/260.
OpenAI Decisions revised: 372/520 (71.5%); scaffolding 210/260; rigor 162/260.
Luna one-word revised: 377/520 (72.5%); scaffolding 224/260; rigor 153/260.
Jev earlier: 376/520 (72.3%); scaffolding 181/260; rigor 195/260.
OpenAI Decisions earlier: 386/520 (74.2%); scaffolding 204/260; rigor 182/260.
Luna one-word earlier: 373/520 (71.7%); scaffolding 213/260; rigor 160/260.

How to judge a disagreement
Look for evidence available before the cut: what the student attempted, whether the answer was correct, whether they explained it, and whether they were stuck. Asking for an explanation can diagnose understanding as well as push rigor. A brief helpful prompt can support productive struggle without taking over. Check whether the released category refers to the immediate next move or to a larger teacher-annotated episode. Teacher comments can describe later events that the decision model was not shown.
We found 3 groups with exactly identical released context but opposite reference categories: 70/71; 367/368; 411/412. These are concrete ambiguities for a context-only classifier, not grounds to delete cases or silently change labels. Other teacher-label concerns need case-by-case review. The archive also contains known non-math material; domain flags below reproduce the earlier screen and are not a complete new subject audit.

Teacher hint for review only: This would have been a good time for the tutor to have the student explain why they changed their mind, and to praise the student for taking the time to think through their solution before choosing an answer. This student is clearly a very strong math student, and they are cruising through these math problems without any help or hesitation.  Sometimes when students are so confident in math, they don't get asked to explain their thinking, and then they lose out on the practice of being able to explain clearly.  When they run eventually run into more challenging problems, they are not used to explaining their thinking and it becomes harder for them to ask for and receive help.  The tutor knows that this student is advanced, and can handle math challenges, so I would recommend that the tutor implement some practice of having the student explain how they solved, and/or show how they solved some of the problems.  If the student is frustrated by this change because the student is used to moving more quickly, the tutor could explain that they just want to practice showing work and explaining thinking on some of the problems because these are essential skills for 6th-8th grade math, and the tutor wants to make sure the student is practicing how to show and explain before they get to the harder math in the higher grades.

Other archived raw runs that disagreed: OpenAI Decisions earlier.
Teacher hint for review only: In this situation, the tutor is being appropriately encouraging and is not providing much support because the student is actively trying the problems and seems to be understanding well.  However, I wanted to make a note to the researchers about line 122.  In this line, the problem is described as a polygon with sides labeled 6 ft, 5 ft, 5 ft, 1 ft, and one side with a question mark.  That is only 5 sides listed, and they add up to 17.  6 + 5 + 5 + 1 = 17.  However, in the problem explanation in line 127, it appears that this shape is six sided, and that the 5 known side lengths add up to 25.  This means there must have been an error in describing the problem in line 122.  Maybe there is a missing side length of 8 somewhere?  The student seems to have this missing information because they arrive at the correct answer of 3.  However, the annotators reviewing this work do not have the full information.  When solving this to check the student and tutor's work, an annotator would likely think the answer was as follows:

6 + 5 + 5 + 1 + ? = 23
17 + ? = 23
? = 23 - 17
? = 6

In the answer explanation in line 127, the system states that 5 side lengths add up to 25 ft, and the perimeter is 28 feet (not 23 feet as it was stated in line 122).  This changes the problem significantly.
